The Committee on Economic Affairs of the Riigikogu discussed this draft on 23 February. The draft was presented to the members of the Commission by Jaak Aab, Minister of State Administration, and the civil servants accompanying him. Members of the Commission also had questions about the content of this draft. Kristen Michal, for example, wondered how the software was still shared between public authorities. Of course it has been done, but there has been no such common practice and a set of rules as to how to do it so far. The purpose of this draft is, among other things, to create [the rulebook] so that public authorities can easily share the software by agreeing on it. The question of possible security threats in connection with the opening of software codes was also raised. In response, it was clarified that the draft provides that if the granting of state software to the public for free would have a detrimental effect on the State, this would not be the case. Account shall be taken, inter alia, of the cyber security risk inherent in the provision of national software to the public, the specificities of the use of national software and the potential threat to public policy and national security arising from the provision of such software. It is also thought later that there may have to be such an additional filter between before the code goes up into the code repository. There was an interest in whether this bill would solve the problems of reconciling different local government programmes in any way. In response, it was said that, yes, rather, this bill provides the basis for one sovereign asset manager to provide another sovereign asset manager with the software that it has to manage, without any greater bureaucracy, and it also creates the potential to avoid such double procurement situations. There was also an interest in the fact that since this software was put into service in a decision-making and voluntary way, how it would work in practice and who today has the most national software. There were also questions about the financing of the code repository. In principle, this law creates the possibility of making software available to the public, and each asset manager himself must look at and assess which of them is possible and what it is intended to make publicly available. It is likely to begin in the area of government of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications, as the initiative has come from there. As regards the financing of the code repository, the aim is to try to make this financing model more sustainable in the future. However, this draft does not explicitly provide that software must always be published in code stores. The institution may also publish it on its website, for example, or publish a code repository and place a reference to it on its website. At the Commission meeting, it was decided to put the draft on the plenary agenda today, 10 March, and to propose that the first reading be closed. Thank you for your attention and for your time.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U19	We will go to today's 11th item, which is the first reading of the draft law amending the competition law initiated by the Government of the Republic and, in connection with it, the amendment of other laws. I'm asking for Jaak Aabi, Minister of State Administration, as a chair waitress. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U20	Thank you, Mr. Chairman. Good Riigikogu! I will introduce you to the draft law amending the competition law initiated by the government and, in this connection, to the amendment of other laws. I am not going to open all the amendments proposed by the bill in these details. The members of the Riigikogu, as well as the committee, have been familiar with them, but here I mention the most important of these [amendments]. Let us start with the main objectives of the draft. The main objective of the draft is to provide a clear national legal basis for the recovery of unlawful and misused State aid in a situation where neither the European Commission nor the Court of Justice of the European Union has taken such a decision. It is important to note here that, even now, a Member State has an obligation to recover illegal State aid on its own initiative, but there are no uniform rules on how to do so. So far, recovery procedures have been based on the regulations laid down in sectoral laws, which have proved inadequate in practice. As a result, the amendment ensures that the recovery of unlawful State aid is based on a single legal act for all grantors of State aid, i.e. the competition law, and also regulates the time limit for the recovery procedure, as well as the limitation period. The draft also provides for the basis for calculating the interest to be recovered. The State aid to be recovered, together with interest, must be recovered without delay, but no later than four months from the date of receipt of the recovery. In exceptional and justified cases, the grantor of State aid may extend the repayment period. The State aid grantor has the right to recover unlawful or misused State aid within a period of 10 years. The deadline shall apply from the date on which unlawful or misused State aid was granted to the recipient of the aid, either as individual aid or under an aid scheme. Recovery of unlawful or misused State aid is an enforceable document within the meaning of Article 2 (1) (21) of the Code of Enforcement Procedure. The amendments should allow for a uniform recovery procedure in the event of the detection of domestic unlawful and misused State aid and in accordance with the rules applicable to the European Commission procedure. In addition, this draft makes some further changes to the competition law of a more precise nature, which improves legal clarity. According to them, the law explicitly states that where State aid covered by a de minimis or block exemption is granted under an aid scheme, the aid scheme must refer to the relevant European Commission Regulation. The aid scheme must also set out the relevant general principles in order to ensure transparency and legal clarity in the granting of the aid. The draft law also provides a terminology specification concerning the replacement of the reference to the Court of Justice by a reference to the Court of Justice of the European Union. On the proposal of the Ministry of Social Affairs and the Unemployment Insurance Fund, an amendment to the Labour Market Services and Benefits Act has also been included in the draft following the coordination round, complementing the law by the possibility of granting State aid to an undertaking in difficulty by way of derogation. The amendment is driven by the latest amendments to the General Block Exemption Regulation adopted by the European Commission on 2 July 2020 allowing the granting of block exempted State aid to undertakings which were not in difficulty on 31 December 2019 in relation to the outbreak of the COVID-19, but which were in difficulty between 1 January 2020 and 30 June 2021. However, given the current mitigation of State aid rules, this date may not yet be final. Thank you for listening.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U57	Thank you. First of all, this first part. Indeed, these rules differ from one municipality to another. In Pärnu, for example, we made chip mandatory last year, before it was not mandatory. But the obligation to keep records of pets exists in any case and the municipalities probably still perform it somehow. Some of them, for example, hold a notebook where [an resident of a municipality] can be recorded as having, for example, a cat at home. Very many people, of course, do not do this, but at least the municipalities have fulfilled their obligation. Now, creating a register. There may not have to be a new register, because today there are three major registers in Estonia, each using more than ten municipalities. If one of these registers is selected, its use is likely to become more advantageous in the sense that a particular municipality has to pay less than it has paid so far. Now, there is no current experience, but one of these registers was set up with state funding and it cost me a year to make a mistake, but I think it was definitely at the beginning of the century, it could have been 30,000 kroons in 2004, which is about 2,000 euros. There is probably no register for this kind of money today, but there will certainly not be hundreds of thousands of [registry] maintenance per year. It is still a very modest IT project. In this sense, of course, I share some scepticism that in recent years the government orders of ministries have not been very exemplary, at least in the IT field. But I hope that this competence will continue to grow, that 15 years of unfinished work will be done, and that in the end, everyone will save money. We are certainly not talking here about annual maintenance costs of EUR 250000, rather it remains what it is now.

Extract from the meeting of the Committee on Rural Affairs of the Riigikogu, which took place in Tallinn on Monday 22 February, Protocol No 89. The meeting was attended by the Minister of Rural Affairs Urmas Kruuse, the Deputy Secretary-General of Food Safety Hendrik Kuusk, the Deputy Secretary-General of Fisheries Policy and External Relations Siim Tiidemann, the Deputy Chancellor of Agriculture and Rural Policy Marko Gorban and the Adviser to the Minister of Rural Affairs Evelin Oras, and the representative of the initiator of the draft, Andrei Korobeinik. We discussed the proposal of the members of the Riigikogu, the same one that Andrei Korobeinik has already read. Andrei Korobeinik introduced the draft initiated by the members of the Riigikogu by 307. He pointed out that the purpose of the draft was to establish a national pet register. He gave an overview of this and, as the initiator's rapporteur, presented the content of the draft in detail. Hendrik Kuusk gave an overview of the position of the Ministry of Rural Affairs. Merry Aart expressed the opinion that before making a decision, it was important to consider why responsibility would be taken away from local authorities. Andrei Korobeinik answered Merry Aart with the same words, as he said here today to the members of the Riigikogu, that it does not take responsibility, but simply makes the work of local governments easier and allows the creation of a national register. Andrus Seed asked whether the new government should be consulted on the draft. Councillor Marin Daniel explained that draft 307 was the opinion of the outgoing government and that there is no need to ask for a new position of the government, as this is an initiative and proposal of the members of the Riigikogu. Andrei Korobenik pointed out that the problem with the national pet register has been up for 15 years. He agreed that it was positive that the Ministry of Rural Affairs had started developing the draft plan. Procedural decisions were also taken. It was decided to propose a draft 307 on the plenary agenda by 8 March 2021 (consensus), a proposal to terminate the first reading of draft 307 (consensus), a proposal to appoint Tarmo Tamm, Chairman of the Committee on Rural Affairs, as rapporteur. At the next meeting we changed the waitress, appointed Martin Repinski a waitress, but as it turns out, Martin Repinski could not report today. All the decisions were consensual: Merry Aart, Kaido Hoevilson, Ivari Padar, Martin Repinski, Üllar Saaremäe, Andrus Seede, Tarmo Tamm, Aivar Viidik. That's all for short. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U192	Our 13th item on the agenda today is draft No 338 of the Riigikogu's decision to amend the decision of the Riigikogu on "Construction of the Problem Committee on Resolution of the National Crisis." The first-reading report is made by Mart Võrguship on behalf of the initiators. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U193	Ladies and gentlemen, On 15 February, on behalf of the Group of the Estonian Reform Party and the Estonian Centre Party, I submitted a draft to the Riigikogu procedure "Revision of the Riigikogu's decision "Construction of a problem committee for resolving the public crisis'. The purpose of the draft is to terminate the committee before the deadline laid down in the decision setting it up, according to which the mandate of the committee ends at the end of the term of office of the Riigikogu. This commission has been set up by the Riigikogu's decision on the establishment of a problem committee for the resolution of a public crisis, which was adopted on 30 May 2019. The task of the problem committee in resolving the population crisis is to design the Estonian population policy in order to ensure the preservation of Estonian nationality, language and culture throughout the ages. The preservation of the Estonian nation, language and culture, I dare say, is the task of all of us, not just one committee, and this is also expressed in the preamble to our constitution. Thus, demographic issues need to be dealt with in a much wider way and in different areas of life and policy-making. The permanent committees of the Riigikogu, mainly the Social Affairs Committee, deal with matters relating to the population. The Ministry of Social Affairs also deals with the same subject matter. The Ministry of the Interior deals with population policy and sustainable family policy. Under the leadership of the Ministry of the Interior, the development plan for the population and a coherent society has begun in autumn 2020, which aims, among other things, at a family- and child-friendly environment, birth rate, health and regional development support and inclusive Estonia. The Committee on Population Crisis in the Riigikogu duplicates the work of the Social and Cultural Committee of the Riigikogu and the Ministry of Social Affairs, Internal Affairs, Education and Foreign Affairs. Therefore, the existence of a problem committee is not necessary, reasonable or economically feasible, as the Commission's work also requires significant additional expenditure from the state budget. In a situation where the aim of the state is to cut public spending, finding savings by the Riigikogu is a necessary example. In January 2021, the position of Minister of Population was also abolished, and officials were transferred to ministries where the relevant policies were previously dealt with. The proposal is to terminate the Demographic Crisis Problem Committee and to continue to deal with the relevant content issues in the Riigikogu, in particular the Social Affairs Committee, the Ministry of Social Affairs and the Ministry of Internal Affairs, Culture and Foreign Affairs. I ask you to support the draft that has been submitted and I am waiting for questions.

It is therefore our task to reduce the difference between the number of children we want and the number of children actually born. This does not just mean saving families from poverty on a need-based basis, but creating the best environment for those who would like to become mothers and fathers by means of universal measures. These measures include housing support, flexible and close-home childcare, child support, which is progressing with the number of children and increasing the participation of both parents. Secondly, when we talk about mortality, we have formulated the main objective of increasing the number of healthy years of life. Perhaps there is also a need to introduce and continue to support a number of measures where one of the major challenges is to increase the mental health and psychological well-being of the public. It is quite safe to say that the cause of death in Estonia is the wrong decisions of people themselves: overeating and drinking, excessive use of all kinds of drugs, lack of exercise, stress, depression, road deaths, suicides and overdoses, which are clearly at the forefront of European countries. All of these are primarily symptoms of mental processes. We need to help people reach better, less damaging decisions for themselves. Thirdly, it is quite clear that in today's world, people are moving more and more across national borders. We know that hundreds of thousands of compatriots live outside Estonia. Our aim must be to maintain diverse ties with people living outside Estonia. Fourthly, for our country, all people living in Estonia must be important, regardless of their native language. There are many among us who were born in Estonia and whose native language is Russian. I do not think it is right for us to show them a condescending attitude, as our President recently did. By understanding and caring, we can achieve more together. The survival of the Estonian population is important for the Centre Party and we intend to continue to defend the population's bottlenecks. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U319	Today's 14th item is the number 335 of the draft decision of the Riigikogu of the Riigikogu on "Construction of the Problem Committee on the Development of the Estonian Language" submitted by the Group of the Estonian Reform Party and the Group of the Estonian Centre Party. The initiators will present a report from the Mart Võrguship. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U320	Also on 15 February, on behalf of the Estonian Reform Party and the Estonian Centre Party Group, I submitted a draft amendment to the Riigikogu's decision "Construction of the Problem Committee on the Development of Estonian Language Learning." On 30 May 2019, the problem committee for the development of Estonian language learning established by the decision of the Riigikogu has been formed on the principle that one member of the committee belongs to each political group. The purpose of this draft is to amend the principle of the composition of the Commission and to establish it on the basis of the principle of proportionality in the future. In accordance with the principle of proportionality, the establishment of a commission would be similar to the principle of establishing permanent committees of the Riigikogu and would also ensure the representation of members of the Riigikogu in the Committee of Problems, in the same way as the composition of the Riigikogu, therefore according to the will of the elector. It is also important to follow the practice and principle that the current Riigikogu has chosen at the beginning of its membership with its organisation of work. The draft decision to set up a Trouble Committee on the Learning and Development of Estonian Language has been initiated by three of the five political groups represented in the Riigikogu at the beginning of the XIV Riigikogu, the Group of the Estonian Conservative People's Party and the Group of the Isamaa, who also formed a coalition. By assigning one member from each political group of the Commission, this choice and the decision also ensured the superiority of the members of the coalition within the committee. In changed circumstances, where two of the five political groups of the Riigikogu have formed a coalition, the principle of establishing a committee established by a Commission decision made in 2019 no longer ensures the representation of coalition and opposition members in the committee on the same principle. Thus, both the principle agreed at the beginning of this Riigikogu is that the majority of ambassadors on the committee of problems are justified and necessary to change the principles of its formation in order to ensure the effective organisation of the committee's work. Please support the bill! I'd be happy to answer your questions.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U373	The Constitutional Commission discussed the draft on Tuesday 23 February. The contents of the bill were introduced by Mart Võrguship, just as he did in this Chamber a moment ago, and I will not repeat his words again, it was the same on a large scale. Urmas Reinsalu and Kalle Grünthal have examined why the parliamentary practice so far is being changed and what is the broader aim of such a change. Urmas Reinsalu also pointed out that in the past these committees had been regarded as not so much legislative as monitoring committees, so it has not always been found that the coalition should necessarily have a numerical advantage. In response, Mart Võrguship explained that parliamentary history supports both methods of solution, either in such a way that each political group has one representative, or is proportional to the representation, stressing that this is the right of the Riigikogu to regulate itself how the membership of committees is appointed. This is our freedom and the freedom of every formation. Paul Puustusmaa considered that the coalition could consider leaving the control committees as opposition-dominated committees. In response, Mart Võrklaev said that this was already the choice of the original coalition of the composition of the Riigikogu, the Centre Party, the Fatherland and the EKRE, and then the problem was not seen to prevail over the coalition. Urmas Reinsalu drew attention to the fact that although formally this is not a precedent, it could still become such a precedent. In addition, it examined how large the committees would be if proportionality had been ensured. Mart Võrklaev explained in response that the competence for determining the size of the composition remains with the Board of the Riigikogu. Toomas Kivimägi agreed that customs and customs have value, but also in the past two years, certain practices were not followed, for example, in the Special Committee on State Budget Control or in the Special Committee on Anti-Corruption. At the same time Toomas Kivimägi agreed that issues concerning the composition of special committees should be discussed again in the wider circle. Kivimägi proposed sending 335 OE to the Plenary Assembly for first reading and then discussing the next three items without taking procedural decisions. The next items concerned the other special committees. Toomas Kivimägi stressed that not all special committees need to be organised in the same way. Kalle Grünthal proposed that procedural decisions be taken on none of the drafts, including the draft in question, and that they be sent to the body of elders for discussion and position. Toomas Kivimägi considered that the most sensible compromise would be to send 335 OE to first reading and, in the case of the rest of the drafts, to take time off and discuss once again what the most sensible composition of the special committees would be. It was then decided to propose that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda by 10 March 2021 (by six people and one against, no abstentions), to propose that the first reading be closed (by four against two abstentions), to propose that the deadline for amendments be set at 24 March inclusive and to appoint Mr Oudekki Loone, member of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs as rapporteur. These last two decisions were taken by consensus.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U386	Thank you. And now I would like to ask for a speech chair as a representative of the Group of Helir-Valdor Seeder, Isamaa. Please! Three minutes extra time.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U387	Thank you. Ladies and gentlemen, At first sight, it may seem that this is an internal technical draft, as the representative of the initiators of the draft tried to argue here. In fact, that is far from the case. This is a very fundamental draft of state law, which changes the parliamentary practice so far. It also changes the mechanisms of parliamentary democracy, which have worked in Estonia on the basis of the same principles during the various coalitions of government and parliamentary formations. If we look at the plans of the coalition and the drafts that have been launched concerning the committees, it is not only the reorganisation of the committees of concern, the abolition of one or the reorganisation of another, but it also concerns other special committees. The aim and main role of all these committees, the National Audit Office's Corruption Committee and the Supervisory Committee on Security Authorities is, in fact, to carry out parliamentary supervision. The exercise of parliamentary supervision is not the same as the main task of permanent committees, i.e. the drafting of draft laws. And here is the principle of parity for the formation of committees. But now we are going to change this monitoring mechanism, which works in a democratic parliament, which has worked through different formations in the Riigikogu, so that, in any case, the coalition will have an advantage in all these committees. Obviously, it has very clear and definite objectives. We know what the status of the Centre Party is now, and many criminal cases are in progress in relation to the Centre Party as a political party or its members. It is necessary, in its own right, that the relevant committee, in the Committee on Corruption, also have an advantage over the coalition. It is very inconvenient if the principle of parity were to function in the State Budget Control Committee. It is good that the coalition can organise the work of the Commission by a majority and decide what is being discussed in the Commission and what is not. This is a very fundamental constitutional approach and a change in practice. We will also add here the draft law amending the Aliens Act, which we discussed here today, which amends the principles of the visa application and the principle of contesting visas, and will do so even on a much larger scale than European law prescribes. Next, the granting of citizenship to persons convicted of repeated criminal offences, the law on hate speech, or restriction of freedom of expression, etc., now making parliamentary supervision meaningless. It is all really such a justice reform or a state reform when we look at this whole pattern. Strangely enough, before the elections, the reform of justice, the reform of the courts and the roll-over of heads were promised by the EKRE, but now we see that the Reform Party and the Centre Party are doing this justice reform in their own way, doing so very strongly. The arguments put forward here today were absolutely not convincing. I don't know if you're going to go forward and deeper with this handwriting and pattern. After all, we currently have institutions operating in this very Parliament, which are still working, on the basis of the law. There is a representative of each political group in the Broadcasting Council. Should it also now be suddenly formed on a proportionate basis, so that the coalition controls not only the restriction of freedom of expression but also what is happening in national broadcasting? I think that's probably the next step. Eesti Pank, [of which the Council] is represented by political groups on the basis of parity. Maybe we'll change that too. As the initiator of the drafts stated, in Estonia, every change in the composition of the parliament will be a new approach to democracy and parliamentary practices, but what we are talking about is not the composition of the parliament, the change in the government coalition, the change in government, the change in government. I think it's very bad. And not only bad, but dangerous. This should definitely be avoided. I very much hope that the initiators of the draft will still think very seriously about creating this precedent, so that there is no temptation to do what has not been so far, so that every change of government will re-paint the entire parliamentary organisation. I heard the chairman of the committee say this: the proportionate approach is that there will be seven members in this committee, the Committee on the Development of Estonian Language Learning. So far, all permanent committees have been set up in accordance with the principle of proportionality. This is due to the Home and Working Procedures Act. This is done by the Board of the Riigikogu. Proportionality has been given a very clear content, it has again been through different formations according to specific rules, and it is still the case today. The Centre Party and the Reform Party, as the two largest, have the possibility to appoint three members to each commission, the EKRE has the possibility to appoint two, the Socialists and the Fatherland one. This principle takes into account the size of the political groups; it is a proportionate approach. And so we get 10 terms together: 3 plus 3 plus 2 plus 1 plus 1 is equal to 10. The interesting approach is now beginning to be: when it was said that there would be a proportional committee of seven, what proportion would that be? This is certainly not a proportionate approach and such a thing cannot be allowed by the Board of the Riigikogu. There are still two options: either a parite approach, which means that there are five members, one from each group, or a proportionate approach based on the electoral mandate given and on the size of the group. This means setting up a committee of ten. Whether each political group makes the most of its potential is up to the group itself, as well as who it sends there, but the size of the committee should come from this logic. In view of all the above and giving the coalition the opportunity, however, to think deeply about whether it is reasonable to come here and implement such a turn in parliamentary democracy in Estonia with such drafts at this time, the Pro Patria Group proposes to reject this draft at first reading. Thank you.

And, of course, as I said in answering the first question, this desire and the need are precisely those sectors that entered the current crisis with much smaller buffers. These sectors, which also did not receive real, effective support last year, are clearly in need of additional support from these sectors. As far as possible, and precisely to those who have been hit hardest, we want to offer it. In addition to the additional budget, in fact, in addition to the extension of the support already decided by the government for KredEx, and in addition to the support measure for tourism directly targeted at EAS, which has already been approved by the government so far, we have the opportunity to use the European Union's crisis aid money, and this opportunity should hopefully open up quite quickly. From this crisis relief fund, too, we certainly want to target the aid specifically to the companies that you have referred to in difficult circumstances. The aim is to support the preservation of jobs and, in addition to jobs, to support the functioning of our health care system, both in the case of the additional budget, in the case of the government reserve fund and in the case of European crisis assistance. Because under what pressure, under what burden the healthcare system is currently working at, it is now at the human capacity threshold, and to the additional costs that have arisen there, we also want to provide support from an additional budget.

I think any person who comes across the border might be able to find some kind of family reason. In this sense, we can make rules, but the question is whether we can ensure that they are respected. The second question is whether the imposition of these restrictions will still result in the spread of this infectious disease being prevented. If we look at this situation now, the spread of the virus within the country is far greater than that of our neighbours. Perhaps this risk will not come to us now from outside, but we will have to deal with this crisis within this country.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U535	Hello, everyone! Can you hear me now?
ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U536	Yes, very nicely. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U537	First of all, one clarification: the draft is the draft of the Social Democratic Party Group and Raimond Kaljulaid. But once again, hello to everyone, both the manager and colleagues! Today, I have the honour to present the draft initiated by our political group and by Mr Kaljulaid. This is a draft law amending the Social Welfare Act 281. The purpose of the draft is to provide a nursing home for any person in need, regardless of the financial means available to him or his family. Our solution foresees that the nursing home becomes available to the elderly who need it for their pension. The price difference is according to the draft to be borne by the state and the municipality, and the development of an exact financing model remains the responsibility of the government. The financing model would apply to both municipal and private nursing homes. Ladies and gentlemen, Social Democrats have consistently stood for the provision of care that would allow the elderly to cope with their home as long as possible and then, if necessary, to live in a nursing home for a pension. After all, one of the most burning problems in our society is that a person who has worked his whole life and paid taxes is not enough pension to pay for living in a nursing home. It has also put an unjustly heavy burden on the loved ones in need of help, and access to the nursing home is increasingly dependent on the size of the elderly themselves, their children's and grandchildren' wallets. This is unhumane and contrary to the spirit of the Constitution. It is the duty of the state to come to the aid of the people here and change the system so that the family does not have sole responsibility. In 2016, the Government of the Republic approved a welfare development plan for the years 2006 to 2023. We read: "Studies show that access to social services is difficult for those in need of help who have family members with maintenance obligations, since in most municipalities the provision of the service is not financed from the resources of the municipality. The person in need may be left without the necessary assistance if the financial situation of the family members does not allow the purchase of the service or if they do not wish to pay for the service. However, it should be taken into account that maintenance obligations often fall on adult children, who may at the same time have a need to support their own underage children." As a result of population ageing, participation in the labour market in the future may be difficult for more people than today because of the care of an elderly family member. The same document stresses the need to find cross-sectoral sustainable solutions to the problem of care burden and says: "More attention needs to be paid to keeping people who are potentially out of the labour market or away from work due to the burden of care or to bringing them back into employment." Our draft offers sustainable solutions here. As has been said, the burden on family members is unjustly high, both in caring for care and in paying for care. The public sector in Estonia spends too little on long-term care compared to other EU Member States, approximately 0.2 to 0.4% of GDP. At the same time, the World Bank has pointed out in its analysis that in order to improve the situation, costs in this area should rise to 21⁄2%. The European Commission's report has also highlighted the underfunding of our nursing homes, the scarcity of places and the unfairly high price level compared to the average pension. As we all know, poverty is high among older people, while the population is ageing. The proportion of people aged 65 and over is projected to increase to nearly 26% of the population by 2040. However, studies show that annual growth in the share of the recipient's own and his family in basic costs and liabilities is increasing. There are currently tens of thousands of people living in Estonia who have to arrange long-term care for their loved ones. Ten thousand are so busy that they have given up their paid work. As Estonian society ages, the number of people in need of care will also increase. According to the Ministry of Social Affairs, in 2018 people paid 53 million euros for the care home service, consisting of the pensions of the elderly themselves and the sums paid by children and grandchildren, which were set to offset the pension and the home fee. At the same time, the state and local authorities spent 14 million euros on nursing home services. In this respect, it is quite appropriate to refer to the following data. In 2008, when the cost of care services was 11 million for the state and municipalities, people paid a total of 17.5 million. In ten years, the cost of local governments had increased by just three million euros, while the amount of money to be borne by people had increased by 35.5 million euros. If we look back in time, we will see that in 2019 the average old-age pension was 480 euros and 746 euros had to be paid on average per month for the nursing home. In some places, the cost of nursing homes was 1300 euros per month, and more than that, it depends on what it was made up of. Children and grandchildren are the ones who currently have to pay the price difference. The share of the recipient and his family in paying for the place of care has increased steadily. While it was 65% in 2010, it has risen to 76% in 2016 and to 80% in 2018. Ladies and gentlemen, Estonia's society is ageing, and in these conditions it is necessary to pay more attention to the dignity of a person's evening of life at the level of the state and local governments, as well as in the private sector. For the most part of the time, we can manage our lives nicely, we are active at a high age, and this helps to maintain social relations, as well as physical and mental health, but at one point outside help is inevitable. We will then face a number of care-related bottlenecks and concerns that need to be resolved. The organisation and cost of care are of great concern to the elderly, and no one wants to remain a burden on their children and grandchildren. The concerns of older people about their future cause them depression, even suicidal thoughts at worst, because for some, the situation is inescapable. Under the leadership of the Social Democrats, we have repeatedly discussed the topic of informal care in this large Chamber of the Riigikogu and have made proposals to improve the situation. We can say that, in any case, it is urgent to develop a modern and comprehensive care system that will allow the elderly to stay in and to cope with their home as long as possible and, if necessary, to live in a nursing home for their pension. According to different calculations, our bill would cost EUR 40 million a year if it came into force as a law. The calculations by the Ministry of Social Affairs show that this would be even less. If we look at other countries, then in developed countries, which we consider ourselves to be, care insurance usually takes place or the amount of a person's own pension is taken into account, with sufficient allowance being left to the person living in a nursing home. If we look at more data from the Ministry of Social Affairs, about 13,000 people use nursing home services each year. As has been said, our financing model would apply to both local authorities, which account for around 60% of service providers, and private nursing homes, which account for 40% of service providers. According to the draft, the maximum amount of own contribution of the person receiving the service is 90% of the pension allocated to him. This means that at least 10% of the pension must remain in the hands of a person living in a nursing home. Finally. In a situation where older people's poverty is one of the largest in Europe and, together with the ageing population, the number of people in need of care is increasing, and services are becoming more expensive, action must be taken now and immediately that the current arrangement, where the child or grandson has no advantage in living his or her life, in order to enable him or her to spend the evening of her life with dignity, is not appropriate for our country. Thank you.

As you ladies and gentlemen remember, both in April and October, the draft decision of the Riigikogu "Proposal to the Government of the Republic to develop legislative changes to solve the long-term care burden" submitted by the Social Democratic Party was discussed in this large Chamber. Helmen Kütt, who introduced the draft, commented and answered the questions, also brought a chronology of long-term care about how long-term care has been addressed. I will also say a few numbers here. Since 2002 the development of the Social Welfare Development Plan started for the years 2005 to 2015 to ensure that health care and social work work go hand in hand; 2015 to 2017 the Administrative Burden Task Force worked; August 2017: the government approves the proposals for financing of primary care provision for the years 2018 to 2021; December 2017: the government endorses the recommendations of long-term care policy and again the work of the Task Force; in 2018 the Ministry of Social Affairs will put together the concept of long-term care on the basis of the work of the Task Force; December 2018: the government confirms this concept, i.e. this concept has already been endorsed; 2019: the Ministry of Social Affairs will undertake a detailed intervention plan on how to build up the long-term care system; and January 2020: the government confirms the plan for the development of long-term care. And by the end of 2020, the Ministry of Social Affairs will present a draft law on long-term care. I haven't seen that intention yet, but we realize it's been a difficult time. That is why I think it is right in the coming years to change the financing of general care services by introducing a model for paying for a component-based service. For this model, the financing of the service is divided into three. First of all, the care service is paid by the local government, assuming that the state contributes 50% of it and the local government pays another 50%. Secondly, the Estonian Health Insurance Fund pays for the care component for the general care service. Thirdly, the recipient himself bears the cost of accommodation, catering and medicine. The financing of long-term care needs to be substantially increased in the coming years to ensure the creation of new services and to increase the volume of services. It is hoped that the European Union will also provide support for the development of services. Separately, the state must lend a helping hand to informal care, ensure competitive pay for the sharing of the most important social resources and for the people involved. There is also a need to further develop services and training that are extremely necessary to support inherent carers. The care system developed over decades is neither fair nor sustainable. We should change it radically. Only in the case of fundamental changes will we be able to guarantee the well-being of people throughout the life cycle. And this should be one of the main goals of each country, of course Estonia's. ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U592	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Today, the draft Act amending the Social Welfare Act at third reading in the Riigikogu solves a number of problems related to the field. Firstly, the possibility of contractual relationships with family parents in family homes is extended in replacement care. Because of the need to provide family-like services, family parents' time is not currently within the framework of the Employment Contracts Act and therefore work must be done on the basis of a contract of command. This, however, does not guarantee the necessary social guarantees for the family parent. For example, sickness insurance is paid out or suspended, but family parents have not been covered by health insurance and the right to a mother to be transferred or released from work when pregnant is not guaranteed or to receive compensation accordingly. And there are other differences. Secondly, the amendment extends the scope for more flexible use of support funds by the secondary care service target group and local authorities. The amendment will ensure greater legal clarity in the direction of aftercare for young people who continue to acquire their education during the post-care period, which is now going to take place somewhat longer. The possibility for local authorities to finance after-care services from the Fund will also be broadened if the young person has interrupted his/her studies after leaving replacement care but decides to continue them after a while. The draft creates the possibility of receiving family benefits, such as child benefit, child allowance for guardianship, single parent child allowance, child-rich family allowance as well as disability student allowance and disability parent allowance, if a child up to 19 years of age is included in the in-service training course of the state institution managed by the Ministry of Social Affairs. The amendment concerns children aged 13 years and over who are not in possession of student status and are therefore deprived of this support. In addition, we are amending the Social Benefits Act for Persons with Disabilities. There, we wish to grant the disabled parent's allowance and the student's student's student's student's education allowance, as well as the child and his or her parent who are included in the list of continuing education courses with inpatient education at the Astangu Vocational Rehabilitation Centre. Parents of disabled children whose child is up to 19 years of age or students with disabilities who study in the adaptation and work training courses of the Astangu Vocational Rehabilitation Centre will also be entitled to benefit. The granting of the right to benefit ensures that people are treated in a dignified and equal manner. The grants will be managed purposefully to families with children who, due to the provisions of the current law, have been sadly deprived of support so far in comparison with the child and his family who continue their studies in the educational institution. As you could see, these are the necessary changes, which are primarily aimed at those people who need more attention. I therefore ask you to support the draft.

I guess this operation and failure of technology are also a part of the R &amp; D that obviously still needs to be developed. The year 2020 was memorable in relation to the pandemic. For researchers, this led to changes in work processes, both in new, urgent research mothers and in the work of less practiced scientific advice. If you look for a positive path, it will certainly be a significant increase in the role of science and the scientific community in solving the concerns of the state and society. The situation of the pandemic has illustrated that countries with a strong and diverse scientific base are better prepared for such surprises. Estonian scientists have also excelled in their ability to react quickly and are among other things valued partners in trans-European networks. I also thank and congratulate the scientific council that advises the government, who has contributed to the COVID crisis. They have been advising the government on a regular basis for almost a year, under the leadership of Professor Irja Lutsar, preparing advice that has had a significant impact on Estonia's life during this period and helped the country to cope with the corona. In addition to the Scientific Advisory Board, several other good people have contributed. The monitoring data collected regularly by Professor Ruth Kalda's team provide a reliable estimate of the virus's spread. Also, thanks to Professor Tanel Tenson and his team, every week we have new data on the presence of the virus in the waste water. This information refers both to the approaching outbreaks of viruses and to their extinction in the region. All in all, based on these studies, we can make knowledge-based decisions in a rapidly changing and difficult-predicted situation. It is difficult to overestimate researchers' research into COVID-19 and its effects in a wide range of areas of life, including security of supply, mental health, education, anti-viral material and socio-economic effects of the crisis. This knowledge is important to us in dealing with the long-term effects of the crisis. Last year's research barometer, which assesses the trust of Estonian people in science, confirms that 89% of the population in Estonia sees scientists as experts. In Germany, for example, this percentage is 64%. We must realise that investment in science is long-term. Today, we are tasting the fruits of our investments yesterday and the day before yesterday, and our decisions and investments today create better conditions for future generations. I would like close cooperation with scientists to continue after the pandemic has been brought to an end, and this is already the case for the next challenges. I am thinking of major challenges, such as climate issues and the associated green turns, which require substantial leadership and must be based on the latest scientific information. In 2020, Estonia's strategy for R &amp; D &amp; I in 2014-2020 ended. In order to ensure a high level of research in Estonia and to increase diversity, the volume of basic research funding as well as research grants, funded centres of excellence in research and supported university development have been increased. In terms of publication, money has been invested, and the efforts of scientists have borne fruit. The number of high-level articles per million inhabitants in 2019 was 2072 which exceeded the target of 1600 articles for 2020. Of all high-level scientific publications, 8.4% are among the world's 10% most quoted scientific publications, which are slightly below the target, as this target was 11%. The objective of protecting the doctorate has also not been met. The goal was 300, but 221 PhDs were protected. We can also talk about how to change this trend. Several innovations have been carried out to increase the social impact of Estonian science. Research advisors have been employed in ministries and branches of companies to carry out research and development activities in these institutions and to organise close cooperation with universities. Scientific advisors are also taking science into policy in all other areas, and the impact of this action is undoubtedly important. In addition, thematic R &amp; D programmes have been developed based on business needs in the areas of ICT and resource enhancement. This resource enhancement programme creates prerequisites for the creation of new, high-value-added products that replace oil-based materials and contribute to health through biochemical enhancement of wood and novel food technologies. In 2019, according to Statistics Estonia, private R &amp; D investments increased by 85.8 million. The private R &amp; D expenditure amounted to €246.8 million. This represents 0.88% of gross domestic product, which, however, is well below the 2% target for 2020. Much of these investments have been made in the information technology sector; no magic wand has yet been found to reach traditional sectors. Productivity has increased rapidly in recent years, reaching 78.8% of the EU average in 2019, but remains below the target. Experts estimate that without the private sector's systematic R &amp; D, it will also be very difficult to get there from here. But that will mean a real test for us, and I'll tell you about it from now on. We also aim to increase the participation and visibility of Estonia in international cooperation in research and development and innovation. Estonia has been active in the European Union's research and development framework programme Horizon 2020 and the volume of contracts won per capita was 241% of the European Union average in 2019. The competence of Estonian scientists has excelled. The choice of Maarja Kruusmaa, professor of computer systems at the Tallinn University of Technology Institute, as one of the seven chief scientific advisors of the European Commission, is certainly a tribute to Estonia as a whole. These seven people give the European Commission expert advice on policy development and decision-making, in other words, they affect the lives of some 446 million people living in the European Union. I also wish Professor Maarja Kruusmaa much wisdom in shaping these decisions or recommendations of great weight. Honourable Riigikogu! Now, let's turn our eyes forward. The great French mathematician Henri Poincaré has written that science is built on facts, just as a house is built up of stones, but an unorganized set of facts is as much a science as a pile of stone is a house. The country's long-term development strategy "Estonia 2035" sets us strategic values. In order to achieve these goals, we must make an effort in every area of life. They are also the basis for the new R &amp; D, innovation and entrepreneurship development plan R &amp; D. For the first time, R &amp; D, innovation and entrepreneurship objectives and lines of action have been integrated into a single R &amp; D &amp; I development plan. This development plan aims at improving the well-being of the entire Estonian society and the productivity of the economy, which is achieved in synergy between science and entrepreneurship. Further development must be based on knowledge-based innovative solutions supported by high-level, effective and diverse Estonian science and a favourable business environment. Science must walk in the same foot as society and its needs, taking the lead. "Today's research must address tomorrow's needs," as Professor Jarek Kurnitski of Tallinn University of Technology has emphasised. Much emphasis is placed on the knowledge transfer of the development plan, which is cooperation between companies and universities. This brings science closer to the economy and society. Under the limited resources of a small country, it makes sense to prioritise your activities. Thus, special attention is paid to Estonia's development needs in five focus areas. These are: firstly, digital solutions in every area of life; secondly, health technologies and services; thirdly, the enhancement of local resources; fourthly, smart and sustainable energy solutions; fifthly, a viable Estonian society, language and cultural space. Together with the state, businesses and research institutions in these areas, we must all work together to provide the basis for rapid economic growth and, on the other hand, to provide solutions to the more important development needs outlined in the "Estonia 2035" strategy, so that secure digital solutions reach all areas, health services improve and offer personal solutions and we use our natural resources more sustainably by introducing new sustainable energy solutions, and the development of the Estonian language and cultural space would not be forgotten at all. Because if we don't, then no one else will. The numeric metrics make these beautiful goals more specific. In addition to the usual funding targets, the new development plan sets the target for productivity growth at 110% of the EU average. At first glance, a very ambitious target. And that is an extremely ambitious goal, but it is feasible when we think through the whole path of how we achieve it. We will maintain and continue to raise the level of our science and improve the working conditions of researchers, including young scientists, so that we can grow on scientists. Money alone is not enough, but we need people who can use all these opportunities. To this end, we are implementing a doctoral reform to get more young people into doctoral studies and to have whom to implement these great plans. That is what we will achieve, I hope, by improving the incomes and social security of doctoral students and reducing uncertainty. We also encourage the mobility of researchers between the academy and the private and public sectors, without which there will be no transfer of knowledge. Rector Tiit Land of Tallinn University of Technology recently stressed that it is time to come down from the ivory tower. We will achieve more than three times the number of scientists and engineers in the business sector, which is one of the goals of this development plan. Doors to international supply chains will not open for our companies if the processes of companies are not digitised. Similarly, cutting edge companies in developed countries require their partners to work well to achieve climate neutrality and resource recovery. This does not mean replacing any individual raw material or packaging, i.e. reviewing the entire business logic and processes on a value-based basis. Many Estonian companies still have this journey ahead of them. This is a unique opportunity for our scientists: to offer technological solutions to Estonian companies to implement the green turn, to test and develop them in order to provide improved solutions outside Estonia later. New technology alone does not change people's ways of thinking, behaviour and preferences. The green turn will cover all disciplines and researchers, from social and behavioural scientists to engineers. The government is ready to take the initiative on these issues and take the lead in change. We are working to ensure that the European Green Agreement, as a model of thought, reaches the people and entrepreneurs of Estonia, and that Estonian scientists and companies are able to benefit from the new growth opportunities associated with the green turn. We buy less, fix and divide more. Companies adapt their business models to meet these expectations, reduce resource intensity and use clean energy. Behavior scientists confirm that rapid changes are not very human. Together with scientists, we need to think through the whole journey and the solutions to make these changes happen step by step, to maintain a standard of living and to leave no one behind. ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U641	Mr President-in-Office of the Council. Prime Minister! Dear members of the Riigikogu, listeners here and there on the screen! The Estonian people have reason to be proud of their science and researchers. We have a very competitive science internationally, we have many successful research groups, good scientists quoted, our researchers are involved in policy-making and consulting, cooperation with our business is progressing, and with the support of the European Union's Structural Funds, such educational buildings and laboratories have been established in Estonia, which give eyes not only to our neighbours but also to many very well-developed countries. We have centres of excellence, doctoral schools, schools of study and research brought in a wealth of good researchers with the help of the Structural Funds, new study programmes have been created. However, there are many things that cause concern. The Prime Minister's presentation showed that the government understands the importance of science. I'm sure it's important. But many problems have been ravaged over the years and it is clear that they will not be solved in either one or two years. I will bring out three problems in my report. Estonia's research budget is highly dependent on the structural resources of the European Union and, unfortunately, Estonia does not always support its own development needs and balanced scientific development. We do not have an exit plan from the Structural Funds. In the new period, Estonian universities and research institutions will receive 100 million euros less from the Structural Funds for research investments than in the previous period. But the biggest problem is that Structural Fund money has a label. The label is called innovation and smart specialisation. This area is very important. But this means that in the new Structural Funds period, all of our basic research will be left unsupported by the European Union. The Prime Minister mentioned that we have strong basic research. But they're only strong as long as they're strongly supported. Indeed, there are 56 million additional R &amp; D planned in the state budget of 2021, but two thirds of this will be channelled through the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications and other ministries to applied research and to improve the development and innovation capacity of companies. This is clearly necessary. But is there enough for basic research? The good news is that the Minister of Education and Research and the Minister of Entrepreneurship and Information Technology are already planning to discuss in the near future how research institutions and higher education institutions can also apply for funds to enhance the development and innovation capacity of enterprises. The Prime Minister also confirmed the need for university support to carry out the digital and green turns. But the problem with basic science remains. It is said in Brussels that the Member States of the European Union themselves must find money to support basic sciences. However, we know that the country currently has very little money due to the fight against the COVID-19 pandemic. And it is easy to ask whether we need these basic sciences at all, whether we focus only on applied sciences and support only the transformation of knowledge into a commodity. I don't mind innovation. Innovation is very important, it is the use of new ideas and knowledge to implement innovative solutions, but somewhere these ideas and knowledge must come from which they will be implemented. Each application is based on fundamental knowledge of the nature and mechanisms of the phenomenon being investigated. For example, all information and communication technology solutions require basic research, whether in data science or artificial intelligence. Vaccines are also a good example. Vaccines were created on the basis of decades of fundamental scientists' work, which was now operational. In order to reach the COVID-19 vaccines, scientists had to understand how genes work, how information flows from DNA to RNA and from DNA to protein, how RNA viruses and DNA viruses' life cycles work, how they use cells to reproduce themselves, how human immune responses are generated to external invaders, and how the virus enters cells using the protein ACE2 on the surface of the cell. Toivo Maimets, Professor of Cell Biology at the University of Tartu, told me yesterday that if someone had said another year and a half ago that they were studying the surface protein ACE2 and how the virus protein SB1 recognized it, it would certainly have been asked why such esoteric science was used with taxpayers' money and whether Estonian science had no major problems. The moral of this story is that readiness is everything. We never know where the next serious problem facing the world will come from, but a number of scientists who deal with such a first-hand, weird and impractical issue must necessarily exist. Therefore, it is important to water the entire fine art, both basic and applied sciences. Problem two. Indeed, we have had a gap between words and actions for a long time. Although much is said about the importance of science, the making of science in Estonia has not actually been appreciated enough. The salaries of researchers and faculty are not competitive; in applying for competitive research grants from ETAg, obtaining a grant has changed substantially in the lottery. The percentage of success last year was 22%, but in many areas [smaller], for example, in humanitariania 15% and in the technical and technological fields that are important to us 17%. Many successful research groups and entire research trends remain free of funding in the rounds of ETAg research grants year-on-year, even if they receive the highest grades from international reviewers. Last spring, ETAg organised an idea collection to solve the problems associated with the COVID virus, and ideas were collected for certain studies. Scientists came up with 152 research projects. Finally, 13 were financed because the budget was 2.1 million. One can only imagine how much better Estonian society would be today if the research money had been more. This year there are 6.2 million new grants, which is 10 million less than last year. In the previous year, 272 applications for basic research and 141 applications for industrial research were submitted. Three-quarters of them were broke. They're applying again, frustrated. The same low success rate makes Estonian scientists and students feel that doing science is not prospective. As the doctorate does not give the salary supplement outside the academy and even in high state offices the doctorate is not valued, the interest of Estonian young people in going to doctoral studies is reduced. And the third problem. Research and development is closely linked to higher education, and its funding is in a deep crisis. We therefore need to talk not only about funding science but also about funding higher education. That is why tomorrow, in the Committee on Culture, we will launch a key national issue for the sustainability of higher education and research and development. I am hopeful that we can achieve a cross-party agreement on the quality and financing of higher education. Science provides a foundation for education, being crucial to the preparation of specialists with higher education in all areas of life. Science is also a part of culture, it creates the quality of life of people and helps us understand ourselves and the world around us. To sum it up. Research, development and higher education will help us not only to become financially prosperous but also to grow from spirit and to remain a nation and an independent state. The wise people invest in science and education with the hope of seeing better times. I wish us all that wisdom. Thank you.

Absolutely agrees that cooperation in the fields will actually produce results, but at the same time we cannot get over or around the fact that we are a small country. This means that we still have to specialise in something, we simply cannot develop cutting-edge science in all areas. They are therefore priority areas. Of course, scientists must be related to Estonian society, but at the same time we do not want scientists to be the doers only in Estonia, but we also want us to have world-class competition and world-class scientists. In other words, we want our scientists to do very well at the cutting edge. I don't know exactly what's in the EKRE program you were referring to. But in any case, national sciences are important, as I have repeatedly emphasised. If we do not deal with national sciences ourselves, we do not develop Estonian language and culture, then no one else in the world will do it except us. Thank you again for this debate! I would like us to continue to work well together on these issues. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U672	Today's seventh item is the second reading of the Bill 115 of Amendment to § 3 of the Psychiatric Assistance Act initiated by the Social Democratic Party Group. Please waitress the Social Affairs Committee member, Hele Everaus!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U673	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, This is a bill 115. This is a draft law amending § 3 of the Psychiatric Assistance Act, which has gone a long way. A little bit of history. On 3 December 2019 the Social Democratic Party Group initiated a draft law supplementing § 3 of the Psychiatric Assistance Act The Board of the Riigikogu appointed the Committee on Social Affairs as the commission of the draft. The draft aims to change the procedure for providing psychiatric assistance to a person with reduced capacity. The draft was and is motivated by the Chancellor of Justice's finding that the current regime does not allow the provision of psychiatric assistance to a sufficiently mature young person under 18 without the consent of his legal representative or without the permission of the court. The first reading of Bill 115 was completed on 21 January 2020. The deadline for submitting amendments was 4 February 2020 and two amendments were received from a member of the Riigikogu, Helmen Küt. The lead committee proposed one amendment to the draft. The Social Affairs Committee also asked for opinions from various interest groups and the Estonian Chamber of Disabled People and the Estonian Association of Youth Associations sent supporting opinions on the draft. The Ministry of Social Affairs also sent its opinion. The Government of the Republic discussed the draft at its meeting on 9 January and supported the purpose of the draft, according to which sufficiently mature persons with reduced capacity for consideration have the right to seek mental health care without the consent of a legal representative or guardian. The Government of the Republic considered it necessary to supplement the draft in such a way that the right to psychiatric assistance granted to persons with reduced capacity for minors is extended to adults with reduced capacity on the basis of their own free and informed consent. The Ministry of Social Affairs also submitted a specific draft proposal to the full extent. The Ministry of Social Affairs sent a further proposal to amend the draft on 10 February, which was completed in 2021. The Ministry of Social Affairs supports the addition of § 3 of the Psychiatric Assistance Act in such a way that the specific regulation provided for in § 766 (4) of the Law of Obligations Act already applies as regards patients' rights of persons with reduced capacity, which always takes into account the ability and ability of a person with reduced capacity to make treatment decisions concerning himself. However, the health care provider shall, where appropriate, involve a legal representative or other close person, taking into account the interests and needs of the incapacitated person, who can support the disabled person. The Social Affairs Committee supported the proposals sent by the Ministry of Social Affairs, including the last proposal sent on 10 February this year, and consequently decided to formulate the draft as the one and only but meaningful amendment proposed by the Ministry of Social Affairs. Now, what has happened in the meantime? It is that the Commission has discussed this draft as you understand, the history is long, full 13 sessions. A large number of specialists from all kinds of disciplines have participated in the discussions at Commission meetings. I still read them out, they are all worth it because they have participated in very serious proposals: Head of the Children and Youth Rights Department of the Chancellor of Justice, Andres Aru, Chairman of the Management Board for Family and Tradition Protection, Varro Vooglaid, Head of the Children's Mental Health Centre, Anne Kleinberg, Head of the Medical Family Counselling Centre, Head of the OÜ Medical Family Counselling Department, Psycho-sexologist Imre Rammul, Head of the Child Protection Board of the Estonian Children's Elders Association, Head of the Child Protection Department of the Police and Border Guard Board Liia Kilp, Head of the Estonian Youth Mental Health Movement Birgit Malken, Managing Director of the Estonian Young People's Association Heili Griffith, Director of the Children's Rights Programme at Tallinn Pääsküla School Liika Saar, Head of the Child Protection Department of the Police and Border Guard Board of the South Prefecture of the Police Department, Head of the Estonian Youth Health Movement Birgit Malken, Head of the Health Department of the Estonian School of the Estonian School of Mashor. On 15 February, it was decided, as I have already said, to support the proposals and to appoint Mr Everau, before you, as rapporteur for the draft by the Committee on Social Affairs. The amendments were subject to an in-depth discussion on 21 February. There were three of them. The first, as formulated and amended by the Social Affairs Committee, is paragraph 1. It lays down the basic principle of psychiatric assistance: psychiatric assistance is provided on a voluntary basis. Paragraph 2 regulates the right of a disabled person to seek psychiatric assistance in the same way as other health services. There have been a number of debates on this, but in fact it is a very important step forward in order to really get all the help we need. Paragraph 3 provides that the health care provider shall involve a legal representative of a person with reduced capacity or any other adult capacity entrusted by a person with reduced capacity, where this is in the interests of a person with reduced capacity. Paragraph 4 states that psychiatric assistance may be provided without the consent of the person himself if the criteria for psychiatric assistance that are beyond the control of the person are met, in accordance with § 11 of the Psychiatric Assistance Act and § 17 of the Psychiatric Assistance Act, and, in the case of compulsory psychiatric treatment prescribed by the court, in accordance with § 17 of the Psychiatric Assistance Act. This first amendment was subject to consensus: with the exception of one abstention, all members of the Commission voted in favour of it. Amendments 2 and 3 tabled by Mrs Helmen Küti, a member of the Riigikogu, were decided to support the substance. Once again, one member of the Commission was impartial; the others were in favour. And, in essence, all these proposals have been taken into account in this only final amendment. The Committee on Social Affairs proposed that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu for a second reading by 10 March, that the draft should be closed, and if it does so, that the Commission's proposal to put the draft on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu for a third reading by 17 March 2021. All procedural decisions were consensual. Thank you.

So far, this was not possible without parental consent. It also states: "Parent and timely medical intervention contributes to effective and effective treatment, which in turn helps to reduce young people's mental problems. " Notice? The focus is primarily on medical intervention, or treatment, without the knowledge of the parent. Misunderstandings with friends, unhappy love, or overly high demands are also common reasons why a young person's mental health is fluctuating. The most common is, of course, Netikius, which remains hidden, but may be the most devastating for a child or a young person's psyche. Yes, the result, i.e. depression or anxiety, can be treated medically with antidepressants and potent sedatives prescribed by a psychiatrist. The baby will be diagnosed, the drugs will make it more dull and less susceptible. However, it does not lose the cause of anxiety disorder or depression. The drugs don't change it, they don't undo it. All such problems require support and cooperation from the whole family, support from your family. It is therefore important that parents are involved in the process rather than being driven away and acted behind their backs without their knowledge. Many of these children have been given antidepressants and sedatives on their own initiative by caring officials. Do you think it's helped these kids? No! Quite the opposite. The pills have given them the opportunity to abuse them or engage them in business, and this pushes the children further down the stairs. Why am I saying this? Because all such problems must be solved not by going to a psychiatrist to receive treatment, or medication, but by identifying the root cause. As several specialists have pointed out, young people need to talk to some specialists first. And for that, we have a wide network of therapists, psychologists, counselors, supporters. When the leg hurts, it does not immediately cut off the leg, but it begins to examine the cause of the pain. Yes, everyone has a right to health protection. But is administering pills really a health protection? How much responsibility, how prudently will a mentally unstable child be able to decide on matters relating to his or her health, as this bill requires? Even adults can't do it in this state.
