ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U720	Mr President-in-Office of the Council. Ladies and gentlemen! Today's second-reading draft law 115, the draft law amending § 3 of the psychiatric assistance act, is a draft with a very long history. I'll name a few dates. On 3 July 2019, the Chancellor of Justice will address the Social Affairs Minister Tanel Kiige with a proposal to change the situation in which a sufficiently mature young person under 18 years of age, who can be considered, is unable to contact a psychiatrist without the consent of a legal representative or without the permission of the court in order to obtain psychiatric assistance. Five months goes by, nothing. On 3 December 2019, the Social Democrats will launch a draft to put an end to this unconstitutional situation. The Board of the Riigikogu appoints the Social Affairs Committee as the lead committee. 9 January 2020 The Government of the Republic is discussing and supporting the draft Social Democrats, but it also considers it necessary to add an extension so that this possibility is also available to adults with reduced capacity. Of course, the initiators agree with this addition. 21 January By 4 February, by the deadline for amendments, no political group or member of the Riigikogu, with the exception of the speaker before you, will submit any proposals. I will now read out a series of dates from 2020: April 20, April 27, April 29, April 30, June 1, September 14, September 28, and October 12. On all these eight days, my proposal as a member of the Commission to put a draft on the agenda of the 115 meetings of the Social Committee was voted down in the Committee on Social Affairs. I have always been of the opinion that the debate is better than the silence, but with 5 votes against, 4 in favour of this proposal was not supported eight times. On page 3 of the explanatory memorandum to the draft under discussion today, there is a list of dates. I don't read them. But for the sake of the verbatim report, there were eight dates in 2020 and five in 2021. These are the meetings of the Committee on Social Affairs when this draft was discussed. And if the date of initiation is also taken into account, it can be said that this draft has been discussed 14 times. More dates. On 15 June 2020, 36 organisations for the mental health of young people sent a joint letter asking the Social Committee to support this draft and to send it quickly to the hall for second and third reading. On 14 December 2020, the Estonian Central Party, the Conservative People's Party and the Group of Isamaa initiated the draft Act amending § 3 of the Psychiatric Assistance Act. On 19 January 2021 the Centre Party and on 21 January the Isamaa Group made a statement in which they wished to withdraw from the three drafts submitted jointly on 14 December as initiators. What's changed since then? Perhaps the most decisive draft, hopefully, to become a law is that in the meantime the government has changed and today's Prime Minister considers the mental health of children and children's rights to be important. That is why the draft procedure initiated by the Social Democrats is taking place in the Commission, it is also moving to a large Chamber and perhaps it will find a solution. I am very grateful to all the dozens of organisations, the best experts in the field, who sent their supporting opinions and rushed to send the Commission a draft to the Chamber for second and third reading. My special thanks and deep bow to the Head of the Children's Mental Health Centre, Anne Kleinberg, a child psychiatrist who practically became a member of our committee, who attended almost all the meetings of the Commission where draft 115 was discussed, answered questions tirelessly and did a public-initiative enlightenment. Thank you so much! Finally, I would like to quote the Estonian Express of 13 January this year, where the partner of law firm Ellex Raidla, Ants Nõmper, writes in an article entitled "Children is also a person": "According to the Constitution, everyone has the right to health protection. An act that essentially deprives a teenage child of the possibility of being treated is unconstitutional and consequently void. The viral pandemic has further aggravated psychiatric problems. However, the Riigikogu does not rush to defend children's human rights, and the political compromises proposed are aimed not at solving the problem but at concealing it." I sincerely hope that the dates of 10 March and 17 March will be great dates and this long history, the length of the text short, but a very important draft will gain the power of the law and eliminate unequal treatment. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U721	Thank you. Siret Kotka will have the floor on behalf of the Centre Party Group. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U722	Head of a good sitting and good members of the Riigikogu! The law on psychiatric assistance has been in the proceedings of the Riigikogu for a long time. This is entangled in the question of whether psychiatric assistance could be given to a minor child without the parent's knowledge and parental consent. In practice, most cases are where parents are aware that their child is attending a psychiatrist's appointment, with the support and coordination of the parent. Unfortunately, there are situations where young people do not have a supportive family environment. The amendment introduced by this Act allows a person with reduced capacity to receive treatment at his/her own request. To this end, the treating physician assesses the child's ability to consider, explains the treatment to the child and should obtain the consent of the child. If, in the opinion of the doctor, the minor is not sufficiently prudent to decide on his/her own treatment, a parent or other person who is trusted by the minor must be involved with the consent of the child. The goal is to get the best treatment for the child. If a young person who is legally incapacitated moves to another city to study and resides with grandparents or other relatives and needs psychiatric assistance, this allowance can be granted by a grandparent or a relative. At the same time, I think it is right that we strive for healthier families, where relations between parents and children are reliable and the parent is aware of the kind of treatment his or her child receives. A parent's right and duty is to be informed of the mental and physical health of his or her child. I would point out that the amendment still allows parents to see from the information system what kind of doctor the child has been seeing and what kind of medication he has been prescribed. The previous speaker stated that these legislative changes are too slow, but it must be said that politics is sometimes very much the art of compromises, and sometimes it takes more time to reach a good solution. Today we're here. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U880	Draft 303 has been prepared by the Group of the Estonian Centre Party, the Group of the Estonian Conservative People's Party and the Group of the Isamaa Party and it has been initiated on 14 December 2020. The purpose of this draft is to amend the law on psychiatric assistance by establishing the right of a incapacitated person to receive psychiatric assistance at his/her own request or with his/her informed consent. The right to psychiatric assistance is essential for the health of young people. In practice, both children and adults are quite naturally involved in the treatment process, given the patient's desire, circumstances and condition. There are no particular problems in this respect and there is no need for specific regulation. This draft amendment to the law on psychiatric assistance provides assistance to all those who are in a position worthy of treatment beyond their will. Also for those who agree to their treatment or treatment, they themselves and their parents. The above constitute the lion's share of the total patient population. However, the weakness of § 3 of the Psychiatric Assistance Act appears in individual cases when a minor person wants help, needs help and can be treated, but his or her legal representative does not agree. A problem is cases where a parent does not consider a adolescent's return justified and the conditions for applying psychiatric assistance beyond his/her will are not met. However, solving this problem should not make the whole regulation more unclear than before. We are therefore planning to amend the draft by changing the wording of § 3 of the Act on psychiatric assistance. The amendment is made to § 3 (1) of the Act and the amendment provides psychiatric assistance at the person's own request or with his or her informed consent. The amendment is also made to § 3 (2) of the Act, according to which psychiatric assistance is provided to a person with reduced capacity with the consent of his own or his legal representative, in accordance with § 766 (4) of the Law of Obligations Act. A health care provider may provide psychiatric assistance to an incapacitated person without the consent of a legal representative where the legal representative has committed a criminal offence against a person who is incapacitated or for any other valid reason. In accordance with § 766 (4) of the Law of Obligations Act, the rights referred to in paragraphs 1 and 3 of [§ 766] apply to the legal representative of the patient in so far as the patient is unable to consider the pros and cons in a responsible manner. If the decision of the legal representative is manifestly detrimental to the patient's interests, the healthcare provider may not comply with it. The patient must be informed of the facts and decisions referred to in paragraph 1 [§ 766] to a reasonable extent. A minor with discretion must be given the opportunity to decide independently on matters relating to his or her own health. In a situation where a legal representative has committed a criminal offence against a person with reduced capacity, it is essential to give the health care provider the right to provide psychiatric assistance to the person against whom the offence has been committed, regardless of the consent of the legal representative. It may be difficult to obtain consent in such a situation and the treatment will not be available to the person in need. Paragraph 3 (3) is also amended, according to which the health care provider is obliged to include a legal representative of a disabled person. If, for serious reasons, the incapacitated person does not agree to include his or her legal representative, the health care provider shall be subject to the consent of the person himself or herself when providing psychiatric assistance. An effective reason shall be deemed to be the absence of parental care of a person who has become aware of a criminal offence committed by a legal representative against a person with reduced legal capacity, or any other threat to the life or health of a person with reduced capacity, which the health care provider is obliged to immediately inform the relevant authorities. The solution proposed by [the bill] excludes a situation in which a person in need of assistance may be deprived of treatment on the grounds that the legal representative has presented a risk to the health or life of a person with reduced capacity and is not interested in obtaining assistance from a person with reduced capacity. An addition is also made to Article 3 (4) [to be included in the Act], according to which the treatment of a person with a mental disorder without his or her consent is permitted only in the cases provided for in paragraphs 11 and 17 of that Act. There are no proposed implementing acts to implement the draft and the law enters into force in general. On 19 January 2021, the Centre Party Group submitted a declaration of withdrawal from this draft and on 5 February 2021, the Fatherland Group also submitted a statement of withdrawal. That's all. Thank you.

If there is a child with suicidal tendencies, we know that antidepressants may increase symptoms during the first two weeks, or the risk of suicide increases. If the parent does not know that the child is in possession of such medications, or practically tools to carry out his or her intention, then the parent must ultimately also take responsibility, but the child has already suffered harm. We therefore believe that it is reasonable to apply specific provisions in such problematic situations, for example, when it comes to the sexual violence of a parent or when a child is left without parental care. Then it is reasonable to apply specific provisions that allow these children to go to a psychiatrist without the consent of the parent, if necessary, without his or her knowledge.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U978	Ladies and gentlemen, Today, we are dealing with two proposals at once, which are essentially dealing with the same problem. In particular, how to ensure access to psychiatric assistance for a person with reduced capacity. It is important that the current procedure, where a sufficiently mature and considerable young person under 18 is unable to obtain psychiatric assistance without the consent of his or her legal representative or the permission of the court, needs to be changed in line with the needs of our accelerating life. Children's mental health problems are a major concern in Estonia. Already in pre-Corona, 2019, according to the National Institute for Health Development, 9122 young people under 15 years of age attended psychiatric visits due to mental and behavioural disorders. At present, the number of children and young people up to 19 years of age in need of psychiatric assistance is boldly above 10,000 per year. It is difficult to estimate how much this would be if all those in need had access to a psychiatrist regardless of the consent of the parent. Clearly, distance learning does not only lead to new experiences but can also cause mental and emotional tension, anxiety, and even suicidal thoughts. On average, 30 suicide attempts have been recorded among children in Estonia during the year, but this year there were 20 attempts in the first two months. Of course, when a young person suffers from mental disorders or other health problems, this is a family problem. Specialists claim that the support of parents or close relatives is very important during psychiatric treatment. In case of close support, the treatment is faster and more effective. It is difficult to deal with your concerns alone even in an adult person, let alone in children. But even in the case of caring and understanding parents, the child may have some disorder and wants to solve the problem without the parents knowing about it. But once again, it is always better for a child to have support from loved ones who are willing to share his or her concerns. Unfortunately, not all parents are best friends and supporters of their child. Children and women, in particular, are victims of domestic violence. Such children and teenagers must also be able to seek help from outside their home. If a child's mental illness is due to mistreatment by family members or to loss of parental care, both child protection and the police should be informed. We also consider it necessary for people to be taught to notice and inform children who need help. The Estonian Youth Association considers that if the doctor considers the minor to be mature and considerable enough to give informed consent, the young person must have access to psychiatric assistance even without the consent of the legal representative. The UN Convention on the Rights of the Child and the UN Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities provide that every person has the right to high-quality and accessible healthcare. This right also extends to persons with reduced capacity. To enable a person to exercise his or her right to health protection, he or she must be able to decide which health services are provided to him or her. Professional psychiatrists work in Estonia and can be fully trusted. But the problem is, unfortunately, we don't have a lot of specialists in this area, and people have to wait several months before they get to a free reception. Thanks to the initiative of Oudekki Loone, member of the Estonian Centre Party, it was possible to direct additional money to the training of clinical psychologists in the course of last year's budget. This will help to alleviate the problem of access to doctors. The Estonian Centre Party advocates the availability of psychological and psychiatric assistance to all people, regardless of their age, gender and financial means, within a reasonable period of time. At present, psychiatrists are the only specialised doctors to whom parents under the age of 18 must have written consent. This requirement applies since 1997 when the Riigikogu passed the law on psychiatric assistance. The Estonian Centre Party welcomes the fact that a long debate on whether a young person who is mature enough to become aware of his or her problem, address a psychiatrist and undergo treatment will reach real results. The Riigikogu will proceed with a single draft, which is a good compromise in the current situation and which takes into account all the necessary proposals and recommendations. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U981	On our agenda today, item No 9 is the number 328 of the draft law amending the Aliens Act initiated by the Government of the Republic (right of appeal for the judgment of visas). This is introduced by the Government of the Republic by the Minister of the Interior Kristian Jaani. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U982	Mr President of the sitting of the Riigikogu. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! I am pleased to stand before the honourable Riigikogu and introduce you to the draft law on the amendment of the Aliens Act, which was initiated by the Government of the Republic on 8 February. The purpose of the amendment is to provide persons with the necessary legal protection to challenge decisions on visas. The amendment is intended to achieve a situation in which an alien may also appeal to the court after the appeal procedure against a decision refusing a visa, withdrawing a visa, withdrawing a visa, refusing to extend a stay and terminating an early stay. The need to draw up the draft act follows from the judgment of the Court of Justice of 13 December 2017. In particular, the Court found that European Union law obliges the Member States to ensure, in the proceedings against decisions refusing a visa, that those decisions can also be challenged before the courts. This has not been possible so far by Estonia and infringement proceedings have also been opened against Estonia. According to the law currently in force, Estonian law only provides for a two-step internal procedure. Due to the regulation of contesting the decision, an alien can first submit an application to the authority that made the decision, such as the Estonian Foreign Office or the Police and Border Guard Board, and then to the Ministry at the next or second degree. Whether the Ministry of Foreign Affairs or the Ministry of Internal Affairs then checks the formal and substantive legality of decisions according to their respective competences. According to the new draft, following the appeal procedure, an alien has the right to apply to the court and the court proceedings are of a three-step nature. The court shall check compliance with the procedural requirements of the administrative body and the conformity of the appeal with the formal requirements, as well as the substantive legality of the decision. In this context, it is important to note that, for the purposes of the compactness, legal clarity and uniform procedure of the regulation, the right of appeal for decisions on visas has been extended, i.e. it also applies to short-stay visas known as more than a C visa or Schengen visa, as well as to long-stay visas, i.e. a D visa or a visa issued by a Member State. It is certainly important to emphasise two other aspects. Firstly, the lodging of a complaint or the organisation of a hearing before a court for the purpose of examining a complaint does not entitle an alien to stay or arrive. Secondly, the facts and reasons on which the decision is based will not continue to be disclosed to the applicant, but will only become known to him on the legal basis of the decision. The target group for the amendment is aliens who submit an objection to a decision refusing a visa, withdrawing a visa, withdrawing a visa, refusing to extend a stay and terminating an early stay. Approximately 100 to 200 such foreigners a year. In 2019, 259 appeals were lodged at first instance, of which 76 were satisfied. Of the 183 rejected disputes, 15 foreigners filed an objection to the second step. In 2020, 184 appeals were lodged, of which 26 were satisfied in the first instance and 21 were challenged in the second instance, of which six were satisfied. At present, it is not possible to assess exactly how many could then proceed to court. Based on the statistics of the previous years, we can see that the expected number of legal proceedings may increase slightly in the extension of the possibilities of appeal. Thank you for listening. I look forward to your questions.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U983	There are questions. Starts with Martin Helme.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U985	Thank you for your question. The role of the Court of Justice in cases is to interpret European Union law and the interpretation given is binding on all Member States in the organisation of European Union law. The Court of Justice has held that Union law obliges a Member State to provide for a procedure for contesting decisions refusing a Schengen visa, which must, at a certain stage of the procedure, ensure that those decisions can be challenged before a court. Thus, once the Court of Justice has issued such a legally binding decision, it has become a part of European Union law and Estonia must implement the decision as part of the European Union. That is, in short, my answer.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U1036	Thank you for your question. The main argument was that obtaining a visa is not a human right; it is rather such a privilege. That is why the State could decide for itself whether or not to issue this visa and therefore do not need to be able to challenge it through the courts. But this particular draft essentially says the opposite, so that's the difference between a paradigm. So, indeed, your presentation in the committee was very exciting and educational, and it was also reflected in the protocol.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U1155	Thank you. The Fatherland Group has such an interesting but very clearly argued proposal to reject this bill, if unnecessary and not appropriate to our security interests. Raimond had a very correct point when he said that we were really dealing with this porridge in the middle of a coronal crisis. I don't think this mush deserves our attention so much at this time. The right thing is what Andrei said. My attention was drawn to this bill when I was in the office of the Minister of Justice, the highest security officials in Estonia, who said that it would create an extremely dangerous precedent for decisions based on sensitive security information of the state on such a sensitive issue as possible judicial challenge to admission. And what is the question? In practice, the question is that there is a great deal of information in these matters moving through the various security authorities' own information channels and it is not conceivable that some subjective information, as well as any claims that these claims by other national operational services will be defended in court by the Estonian security authorities in judicial proceedings and published by these sources. It's an unrealistic idea. There are three main problems. The first problem, which was well opened by Andrei Korobeinik, concerns that we are creating a category of subjective decision-making for foreigners in public law, not anymore, but also their subjective right. And by doing so, we will make the right of entry a sort of operational phenomenon within the human rights category. This, I think, is not fit for purpose, because it limits the choice of our country, above all, executive power, to a significant extent. Just as in some other areas of life where the state has a subjective decision to consider, whether it is giving the President's orders or any other issue that is not subjective to judicial review in terms of subjective law, so, I think, it should also remain in the case of processing visa applications. The second category undoubtedly concerns the practical security interest, including the situation where, in this case, the practice of security authorities is likely to prevent entry in the interests of security in the case of various hypothetical or estimated, prognostic considerations, in fact, becomes much more narrow and cautious. Security authorities, fearing that they will have to answer to the court through administrative court proceedings, prefer, if they do not have strong, publicly available evidence, to be more cautious and certainly not in the interests of Estonia's security. The problems of the third category, which are ideologically involved in this draft, are, in my view, linked to the fact that persons who do not have the right to entry and who challenge it, in such a case, there could certainly be situations in which [they] may also apply for legal proceedings to enable that court to be held on the territory of the country, and then it will undoubtedly be a purely court prerogative. No executive official or politician, including the Minister of the Interior or the Government, as the author of this draft, can give guarantees as to what the possible case law will become, as the judiciary is independent. Let me give you a memorable example. In fact, when the Russian military pensioners were talking about staying in [Estonia], Eerik remembers this, he dealt with this committee in his own time, the practice of the administrative chamber of the Supreme Court was finally determined in a completely different and broader way. And we can actually reach the same right based on precedents. As far as the processing of this draft is concerned, I have really opposed the procedure of the draft in those years, I have considered it unnecessary and prevent the realisation of Estonian security interests, and thirdly, the creation of an ideological category of human rights or subjective rights on migration issues on issues for which there is no real need, and it is not justified by international human rights standards. And thirdly, as regards the reference to the fact that references, letters from the European Commission have been made to this effect, the task of the officials of the Estonian state is to argue meaningfully, to state the chain of arguments and the category of arguments in respect of which one might say that Estonia has arguments, Estonia will apply these corresponding decisions arising from the European Court of Justice according to the particular nature of its legal order. This is nothing unusual, the protection of national interests, including security interests, in a collective body such as the different institutions of the European Union, all Member States are involved. There is nothing deplorable or inappropriate about this. My proposal, on behalf of the Isamaa Group, is to put this bill as a matter of poor precedents in terms of security, a matter which is constitutionally dangerous and completely unnecessary, in a peacefully moving chamber. We shall vote in favour of rejecting this matter. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U1162	Today's 10th item is the first reading of the draft law amending the State Assets Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. Please waitress Jaak Aabi, Minister of State Administration!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-03-10_U1163	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! It's about time we got into the law on the amendment of the State Property Act. I will introduce you to the draft law amending the State Assets Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. This is a draft that will help us take an important step towards a more open, transparent and business-friendly digital state. The world we live in today is intertwined with information technology and is developing rapidly. Therefore, our legal space must also keep pace with important technological developments. This bill brings one key law, the State Assets Act, into line with the need that the modern information society presents to us. I will immediately get to the more important point and explain what is the need to amend the law. Today, we are in a situation where the current state property law is perfectly suited to the organisation of state-owned movable and immovable property relations. Today, however, in addition to physical assets, the state still owns many important digital assets, much of which is software, such as our numerous information systems, applications, web frameworks and their components. There are no specific provisions in the current law on the organisation of legal relations with regard to digital property and the same provisions governing the physical world must apply. Since software and physical assets are different in nature, the application of the provisions for regulating the physical world to software has resulted in a great deal of time, clumsy procedures and legal uncertainty in recent years. As a rule, physical property can only be used in one place at a time, whereas the same source code of software can be digitally shared in virtually unlimited terms, regardless of location, whereas its use between several parties does not result in the depreciation and depreciation of the property, as is typically the case for movable and immovable property. It cannot be said that the state's software has not yet been made public at all. However, this has been done, with the launch in 2019 of a code repository for publicly accessible e-state co-operation and software recovery, which has also been actively implemented by several institutions, to make it convenient. I've been looking at this code repository page, and at the moment there are, as far as I know, about 90 software solutions for public use. In practice, however, it has become clear that the institutions' understanding of the possibility and means of disclosure of software is very different and uneven. At the same time, many codes have already been used and institutions wishing to do so have repeatedly expressed their wish that there should be a clear legal basis and procedure for the disclosure of software. Once the legislative amendment enters into force, the long-awaited clarity will be created, with the effect that we hope to see much more of the public-private joint developments and the disclosure of the software code. When speaking about the private sector, it is also worth mentioning that Estonia's Information Technology and Telecommunications Union, which brings together IT companies, has been constantly consulted during the preparation of the change concerning the disclosure of state software. The Union has expressed its strong support for the draft insofar as it creates opportunities for the operators of the IT sector to re-use the source code, which has so far often been excluded from public procurement practice. Indeed, in the course of procurement, the company is often not given rights to the software source code to allow it to be re-used. By amending the law, however, we create the possibility for public authorities to allow such re-use. Public disclosure of state software also allows the provision of improvements to the solutions used by the state and the development of completely new products and services. Thus, the State Assets Act will be supplemented by a new separate section which will enable public authorities to make the source code of the software available to the public. Limitations and conditions under which the use of national software is not authorised shall be provided. There are also grounds for the simultaneous use of state software by several state property managers. Disclosure of the source code of the national software does not entail a significant additional workload for public authorities. The draft does not create more bureaucracy, nor does it create mechanisms for filing, settling and contesting applications in any form. The software shall be made available to the public for use on a free-will, uniform and non-discriminatory basis by the sovereign. It also mitigates the risks of State aid and the risks of recovery of funds requested from the European Union's Structural Funds. There is no selective or anti-competitive effect and the software is made available to the public for unlimited and free of charge. If a sovereign asset manager decides to disclose state software, it shall do so on the basis of the licence terms of its choice. In particular, non-exclusive, widely used standard licences shall be used. However, the draft also sets out restrictions and conditions under which the use of national software is not granted, for example where there is a risk to national cyber security, public order or security. This is a mandatory prerequisite that a sovereign asset manager must take into account, analyse and reach a conclusion on the existence or absence of potential adverse effects before considering granting state software to the public. Before publication, the above criteria are also assessed by the State Information System Authority, which manages the code repository. In addition to the disclosure of the software code, the draft also facilitates the possibility of sharing software between public authorities themselves. The current law provides that if two sovereigns wish to use the same state assets, one sovereign must transfer the state assets to another institution. However, as I mentioned earlier, this is irrelevant to software. The same source software can mostly be used by both institutions at the same time without disturbing either. As you can imagine, such provisions for regulating physical property, which must now be applied to software, have resulted in a considerable amount of cumbersome bureaucracy. Therefore, the present draft provides for a simple procedure based on an agreement between two state assets managers instead of the current transfer procedure in the case of software. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! To conclude, once again, with this bill, we are taking an important step towards a more transparent, simpler and logical digital state. I would like your support for the bill. Thank you!
